[2401.07018] Least squares for cardinal paired comparisons data Least squares for cardinal paired comparisons data Rahul Singh George Iliopoulos Ori Davidov 1 Department of Mathematics, Indian Institute of Technology Delhi, Delhi 110016, India 2 Department of Statistics and Insurance Science, School of Finance and Statistics, University of Piraeus, 80 Karaoli and Dimitriou str., 18534 Piraeus, Greece 3 Department of Statistics, University of Haifa, Mount Carmel, Haifa 3498838 Israel E-mail: wrahulsingh@gmail.com (R Singh), geh@unipi.gr (G Iliopoulos), davidov@stat.haifa.ac.il (O Davidov) Abstract Least square estimators for graphical models for cardinal paired comparison data with and without covariates are rigorously analyzed. Novel, graph–based, necessary and sufficient conditions that guarantee strong consistency, asymptotic normality and the exponential convergence of the estimated ranks are emphasized. A complete theory for models with covariates is laid out. In particular, conditions under which covariates can be safely omitted from the model are provided. The methodology is employed in the analysis of both finite and infinite sets of ranked items where the case of large sparse comparison graphs is addressed. The proposed methods are explored by simulation and applied to the ranking of teams in the National Basketball Association (NBA). Key-Words : Graph Laplacian, High dimensional inference, Large sample properties, Least square ranking, Linear models, Regression. 1 Introduction There are many situations in which ranking a set of items is desired. Examples include the evaluation of political candidates, sports, information retrieval, and a variety of modern internet and e-commerce applications (e.g., Cremonesi et al., 2010, Buhlmann and Huber 1963, Govan 2008, Barrow et al. 2013, Xu et al. 2014, Cururingu 2015). The theory and methodology of ranking methods have been extensively studied from a variety of perspectives by researchers in the fields of mathematics (Langville and Mayer 2012), economics and social choice theory (Sen 1986, Slutzki and Volij 2005), machine learning (Ailon et al. 2008, Furnkranz and Hullermeier, 2010), psychology (Davis-Stober 2009, Regenwetter et al 2011) as well as many other disciplines. The focus of most of the existing literature, both old and new, has been procedural rather than inferential. For a statistical perspective on ranking methods, of a somewhat different flavor ours, see the books by Marden (1995) and Alvo and Yu (2014). A ranking of a set of items can be inferred from different types of data including scores (Balinski and Lariki, 2010) and ranked lists (Marden, 1995). In particular, paired comparison data (PCD) is obtained if all comparisons involve only two items (David, 1988). Suppose that there are K K items labelled 1 , 2 , … , K 1,2,\ldots,K which we would like to rank. Let Y i ​ j ​ k Y_{ijk} denote the outcome of the k t ​ h k^{th} comparison among items i i and j j . The random variable (RV) Y i ​ j ​ k Y_{ijk} may be binary, ordinal or cardinal. In this paper we consider cardinal, i.e., continuous PCD. We assume that the observations Y i ​ j ​ k Y_{ijk} for 1 ≤ i ≠ j ≤ K 1\leq i\neq j\leq K and k = 1 , … , n i ​ j k=1,\ldots,n_{ij} satisfy Y i ​ j ​ k = μ i ​ j + ϵ i ​ j ​ k , Y_{ijk}=\mu_{ij}+\epsilon_{ijk}, (1) where μ i ​ j = 𝔼 ⁡ ( Y i ​ j ​ k ) = μ i − μ j \mu_{ij}=\mathbb{E}(Y_{ijk})=\mu_{i}-\mu_{j} and ϵ i ​ j ​ k \epsilon_{ijk} are independent zero mean RVs. The parameters μ 1 , … , μ K \mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{K} are refered to as the merits or scores. If we further assume that ϵ i ​ j ​ k \epsilon_{ijk} are IID 𝒩 ⁡ ( 0 , σ 2 ) \mathcal{N}(0,\sigma^{2}) RVs then ( 1 ) is a specially parameterized homoscedastic normal linear model. Clearly this parametrization results in a global ranking referred to as a total linear ranking, cf. Oliveira et al. (2018) and the references therein, in which the merits quantify the degree of preference. We refer to such models as linear models on graphs or more compactly as graph–LMs. It is useful to note that we may view Y i ​ j ​ k Y_{ijk} as the outcome of the k t ​ h k^{th} “game” between team i i and team j j where team i i scored a a points while team j j scored b b points. Then naturally Y i ​ j ​ k = a − b Y_{ijk}=a-b and Y j ​ i ​ k = b − a Y_{jik}=b-a and thus Y i ​ j ​ k = − Y j ​ i ​ k Y_{ijk}=-Y_{jik} . Each item is viewed as a vertex of a graph and the set of vertices is denoted by 𝒱 = { 1 , … , K } \mathcal{V}=\{1,\ldots,K\} . Let n i ​ j = n j ​ i n_{ij}=n_{ji} denote the number of comparisons between item i i and item j j . We do not assume that n i ​ j &gt; 0 n_{ij}&gt;0 for all or even most pairs ( i , j ) (i,j) . If n i ​ j &gt; 0 n_{ij}&gt;0 , then items i i and j j are connected by an edge denoted by ( i , j ) (i,j) . Let ℰ \mathcal{E} denote the set of edges. The structure 𝒢 = ( 𝒱 , ℰ ) \mathcal{G}=(\mathcal{V},\mathcal{E}) is called a graph (Gould, 2012). We note that some authors consider each individual comparison between items i i and j j as an edge. Thus n i ​ j &gt; 1 n_{ij}&gt;1 would imply multiple edges connecting items i i and j j . Such a structure is sometimes referred to as a multigraph. The distinction between a graph and a multigraph is inconsequential from our perspective. With each edge ( i , j ) (i,j) we associate a random sample 𝒴 i ​ j = ( Y i ​ j ​ 1 , … , Y i ​ j ​ n i ​ j ) \mathcal{Y}_{ij}=(Y_{ij1},\ldots,Y_{ijn_{ij}}) of n i ​ j n_{ij} comparisons; the set of all samples is denoted by 𝒴 \mathcal{Y} . We call the pair ( 𝒢 , 𝒴 ) \displaystyle(\mathcal{G},\mathcal{Y}) (2) a pairwise comparison graph (PCG). Least square estimators for the model ( 1 ) have a long history and have been studied in diverse fields (cf. Mosteller 1951, Kwiesielewicz 1996, Csato 2015). Surprisingly, despite the simplicity of model ( 1 ), the extensive associated literature, including the many papers on least square estimators, the statistical properties of the least squares estimator have not been properly studied. This paper fills this gap by developing a graph–based statistical theory necessary for conducting inference on the vector of merits. As shown model ( 1 ), as well as its extensions, exhibit unique features which complicate and enliven their analysis. In particular our contributions are: 1. The large sample properties of the least square estimator for finite comparison graphs are established using novel necessary and sufficient graph–based conditions. The relationship between the topology of the comparison graph and the quality of the resulting estimators is emphasized. A strong law for the merit parameter is established and the derived rankings are shown to converge at an exponential rate. 2. Although some papers on graph–LMs with covariates exist, e.g., Harville (2003) who considered a single binary covariate, to the best of our knowledge this is the first paper to rigorously examine such models. In particular, conditions under which models with covariates can be analyzed are provided and analyzed. Situations in which covariates can be omitted from the model are emphasized. 3. Finally, an analysis of graph-LMs when the number of items compared grows to infinity is provided. It is shown that up to a logarithmic factor connectivity is sufficient for ensuring that asymptotic normality and uniform consistency. This paper focuses on cardinal PCD satisfying ( 1 ) with μ i ​ j = μ i − μ j \mu_{ij}=\mu_{i}-\mu_{j} . More generally, the outcomes Y i ​ j ​ k Y_{ijk} may be binary or ordinal. In binary PCD Y i ​ j ​ k ∈ { 0 , 1 } Y_{ijk}\in\{0,1\} and the probability that item i i is preferred over item j j is ℙ ⁡ ( Y i ​ j ​ k = 1 ) = F ⁡ ( λ i − λ j ) \mathbb{P}(Y_{ijk}=1)=F(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j}) where F F is a distribution function, symmetric about 0 0 , i.e., F ⁡ ( x ) + F ⁡ ( − x ) = 1 F(x)+F(-x)=1 for all x ∈ ℝ x\in\mathbb{R} , and λ 1 , … , λ K \lambda_{1},\ldots,\lambda_{K} are the merit parameters, cf. Oliveira et al. (2018). Specifically, when F F is the standard logistic distribution we obtain the relation ℙ ⁡ ( Y i ​ j ​ k = 1 ) = exp ⁡ ( λ i − λ j ) 1 + exp ⁡ ( λ i − λ j ) = μ i μ i + μ j , \mathbb{P}(Y_{ijk}=1)=\frac{\exp(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j})}{1+\exp(\lambda_{i}-\lambda_{j})}=\frac{\mu_{i}}{\mu_{i}+\mu_{j}}, (3) where μ i = exp ⁡ ( λ i ) \mu_{i}=\exp(\lambda_{i}) for all i = 1 , … , K i=1,\ldots,K . The μ ′ ​ s \mu^{\prime}s are also referred to as the merits. This is the well–known Bradley–Terry model (BT) (Bradley and Terry 1952, Hunter 2004, Cattelan 2012) whereas when F F is the standard normal DF we obtain the so–called Thurstone model (Thurstone 1927, Böckenholt, 2006). Both of these are instances of discrete choice models (Luce 1959, Bierlaire 1998) with a binary choice. The relationship between models ( 1 ) and ( 3 ) will be discussed in a comprehensive manner in Section 8 of this paper. The paper is organized as follows. The least square estimator is introduced and its elementary properties are explored in Section 2. Section 3 investigates the large sample properties of the LSE. Section 4 and 5 extend model ( 1 ) in two directions. In Section 4 models with covariates are analyzed and in Section 5 the number of items compared is allowed to grow to infinity. Simulation results are presented in Section 6 and an illustrative example is discussed in Section 7. We conclude in Section 8 with a brief summary and discussion. In particular, we indicate how the results from Sections 2–7 extend to binary models such as ( 3 ) and beyond. All proofs are collected in the Supplement ( A ) and R-scripts for reproducing all experiments are available at https://github.com/rahulstats/GLM-I . 2 Least squares on graphs: estimation and elementary properties Model ( 1 ) is the simplest possible graphical linear model (graph–LM) in which a global linear ranking is assumed. Although this model has been widely studied in diverse fields (cf. Mosteller 1951, Kwiesielewicz 1996, Csato 2015) it is surprising that a variety of very basic statistical questions have not been adequatly addressed. Consider the objective function Q ⁡ ( 𝝁 ) = ∑ 1 ≤ i &lt; j ≤ K ∑ k = 1 n ij ( Y ijk − ( μ i − μ j ) ) 2 , Q(\bmu)=\sum_{1\leq i&lt;j\leq K}\sum_{k=1}^{n_{ij}}(Y_{ijk}-\left(\mu_{i}-\mu_{j}\right))^{2}, (4) which is nothing but a sum of squares over the PCG ( 2 ) where 𝝁 ⊤ = ( μ 1 , … , μ K ) \bmu^{\top}=\left(\mu_{1},\ldots,\mu_{K}\right) is the vector of merits. Clearly, if for all ( i , j ) ∈ ℰ (i,j)\in\mathcal{E} and k = 1 , … , n i ​ j k=1,\ldots,n_{ij} we have Y i ​ j ​ k ∼ 𝒩 ⁡ ( μ i − μ j , σ 2 ) Y_{ijk}\sim\mathcal{N}(\mu_{i}-\mu_{j},\sigma^{2}) then ( 4 ) is the kernel of the likelihood for a normal linear model. Further note that Q ⁡ ( 𝝁 ) = Q ⁡ ( 𝝁 + c ​ 𝟏 ) Q(\bmu)=Q(\boldsymbol{\mu}+c\boldsymbol{1}) for any c ∈ ℝ c\in\mathbb{R} where 𝟏 ⁡ 1 = ( 1 , … , 1 ) ⊤ ∈ ℝ K \b 1=(1,\ldots,1)^{\top}\in\mathbb{R}^{K} and consequently 𝝁 \bmu is not estimable unless a constraint is imposed. Therefore, we define the least squares estimator (LSE) by 𝝁 ^ = arg ⁡ min ⁡ { Q ⁡ ( 𝝁 ) : 𝐯 ⊤ ​ 𝝁 = 0 } \widehat{\bmu}=\arg\min\{Q(\bmu):\bv^{\top}\bmu=0\} (5) where 𝐯 \bv is a preselected vector satisfying 𝟏 ⁡ 1 ⊤ ​ 𝐯 ≠ 0 \b 1^{\top}\bv\neq 0 , i.e., 𝐯 \bv is not a contrast, cf. Remark A.1 . We require the following notations. For any i ≠ j i\neq j let S i ​ j = ∑ k = 1 n i ​ j Y i ​ j ​ k S_{ij}=\sum_{k=1}^{n_{ij}}Y_{ijk} and S i = ∑ j ≠ i S i ​ j . S_{i}=\sum_{j\neq i}S_{ij}. Further define, 𝐒 = ( S 1 , … , S K ) ⊤ , \bS=(S_{1},\ldots,S_{K})^{\top}, and let 𝐍 = ( N ij ) \bN=(N_{ij}) be the K × K K\times K Laplacian of the graph 𝒢 \mathcal{G} . Its elements are N i ​ j = { ∑ j n i ​ j if i = j − n i ​ j if i ≠ j , N_{ij}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ccc}\sum_{j}n_{ij}&amp;\text{if}&amp;i=j\\ -n_{ij}&amp;\text{if}&amp;i\neq j\end{array}\right., i.e., the i t ​ h i^{th} diagonal element of the Laplacian is n i = ∑ j n i ​ j n_{i}=\sum_{j}n_{ij} which is the degree of vertex i i . The total number of paired comparisons is denoted by n = ∑ 1 ≤ i &lt; j ≤ K n i ​ j n=\sum_{1\leq i&lt;j\leq K}n_{ij} . Laplacians play an important role in graph theory (Bapat 2010). It is easy to verify that they are symmetric positive semidefinite with rows and columns that sum to 0 0 . The vertices i i and j j are said to be connected if there exists a sequence of edges ( i , v 1 ) , ( v 1 , v 2 ) , … , ( v l − 1 , v l ) , ( v l , j ) (i,v_{1}),(v_{1},v_{2}),\ldots,(v_{l-1},v_{l}),(v_{l},j) . Such a sequence is called a path. If all pairs of vertices are connected then 𝒢 \mathcal{G} is a connected graph. Theorem 2.1 A unique solution to ( 5 ) exists if and only if 𝒢 \mathcal{G} is connected in which case the LSE is given by 𝝁 ^ = 𝐍 + ​ 𝐒 − 𝐯 ⊤ ⁡ 𝐍 + ​ 𝐒 𝐯 ⊤ ⁡ 𝟏 ⁡ 1 ​ 𝟏 ⁡ 1 . \widehat{\bmu}=\bN^{+}\bS-\frac{\bv^{\top}\bN^{+}\bS}{\bv^{\top}\b 1}\b 1. (6) where 𝐍 + \bN^{+} is the Moore–Penrose inverse of 𝐍 \bN . The proof of Theorem 2.1 shows that if 𝒢 \mathcal{G} is not connected then there are at least two distinct collections of items I I and J J , say, that can not be directly compared, i.e., the difference μ i − μ j \mu_{i}-\mu_{j} is not estimable whenever i ∈ I i\in I and j ∈ J j\in J . Surprisingly, formula ( 6 ) has not been previously derived; however it was mentioned by Ghosh and Davidov (2020) who studied alternatives to the least square estimator. Typically, it has been implicitly assumed that 𝐯 = 𝟏 ⁡ 1 \bv=\b 1 , e.g., Csato (2015), in which case ( 6 ) reduces to 𝐍 + ​ 𝐒 \bN^{+}\bS . It is also worth noting that most papers on the LSE end here, i.e., they make no attempt at studying the statistical properties of ( 6 ). Further note that ‖ 𝝁 ^ ‖ 2 2 = 𝐒 ⊤ ⁡ 𝐍 + ​ 𝐍 + ​ 𝐒 − 2 ​ 𝐯 ⊤ ⁡ 𝐍 + ​ 𝐒 𝐯 ⊤ ⁡ 𝟏 ⁡ 1 ​ 𝐒 ⊤ ​ 𝐍 + ⁡ 𝟏 ⁡ 1 + K ​ ( 𝐯 ⊤ ⁡ 𝐍 + ​ 𝐒 𝐯 ⊤ ⁡ 𝟏 ⁡ 1 ) 2 . \left\|\widehat{\bmu}\right\|_{2}^{2}=\bS^{\top}\bN^{+}\bN^{+}\bS-2\frac{\bv^{\top}\bN^{+}\bS}{\bv^{\top}\b 1}\bS^{\top}\bN^{+}\b 1+K\left(\frac{\bv^{\top}\bN^{+}\bS}{\bv^{\top}\b 1}\right)^{2}. The second term on the right–hand–side above equals zero for all 𝐯 \bv ; the third term is zero if and only if 𝐯 \bv is in the kernel of 𝐍 + . \bN^{+}. However, if 𝒢 \mathcal{G} is connected then ker ⁡ ( 𝐍 + ) = ker ⁡ ( 𝐍 ) = span ⁡ ( 𝟏 ) \mathrm{\ker}(\bN^{+})=\mathrm{\ker}(\bN)={\rm span}(\boldsymbol{1}) (Bapat, 2010). Thus, 𝐍 + ​ 𝐒 \bN^{+}\bS is the minimum norm solution to the unconstrained minimization problem given in ( 4 ). Next, we investigate the relationship among estimators obtained under different constraints. We emphasize that the choice of the constraint in ( 5 ) is not of great importance since the estimated values of μ i ​ j \mu_{ij} are invariant with respect to the chosen constraint: Corollary 2.1 For any 𝐯 \boldsymbol{v} and 𝐮 \boldsymbol{u} defined in Proposition 2.1 and any 1 ≤ i , j ≤ K 1\leq i,j\leq K we have μ ^ i ​ ( 𝐯 ) − μ ^ j ​ ( 𝐯 ) = μ ^ i ​ ( 𝐮 ) − μ ^ j ​ ( 𝐮 ) . \widehat{\mu}_{i}(\bv)-\widehat{\mu}_{j}(\bv)=\widehat{\mu}_{i}(\boldsymbol{u})-\widehat{\mu}_{j}(\boldsymbol{u}). Remark 2.1 Some authors consider a weighted objective function, i.e., Q 𝐰 ​ ( 𝛍 ) = ∑ i &lt; j ∑ k = 1 n i ​ j w i ​ j ​ ( Y i ​ j ​ k − ( μ i − μ j ) ) 2 Q_{\boldsymbol{w}}\left(\bmu\right)=\sum_{i&lt;j}\sum_{k=1}^{n_{ij}}\\ w_{ij}(Y_{ijk}-\left(\mu_{i}-\mu_{j}\right))^{2} . The weighted model is used to handle heteroscedasticity, i.e., different comparisons carry different levels of information. The analysis of the weighted and unweighted sum of squares is similar. The only difference is that in equation ( 6 ) we replace 𝐒 \bS by 𝐒 w \bS_{w} and 𝐍 \bN by 𝐍 w \bN_{w} where the j t ​ h j^{th} element of 𝐒 w \bS_{w} is ∑ i ≠ j w j ​ i ​ S j ​ i \sum_{i\neq j}w_{ji}S_{ji} and 𝐍 w \bN_{w} is the K × K K\times K weighted Laplacian with elements ∑ j w i ​ j ​ n i ​ j \sum_{j}w_{ij}n_{ij} when i = j i=j and − w i ​ j ​ n i ​ j -w_{ij}n_{ij} otherwise. Since the analysis of the weighted and unweighted cases is similar we shall henceforth consider only the unweighted model. We will assume: Condition 2.1 The errors ϵ i ​ j ​ k \epsilon_{ijk} are IID with zero mean and a finite variance σ 2 \sigma^{2} . Next, we address the statistical properties of ( 6 ). Since 𝔼 ⁡ ( Y i ​ j ​ k ) = μ i − μ j \mathbb{E}(Y_{ijk})=\mu_{i}-\mu_{j} it follows that 𝔼 ⁡ ( 𝐒 ) \displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\bS) = \displaystyle= ( 𝔼 ⁡ ( S 1 ) , … , 𝔼 ⁡ ( S K ) ) ⊤ = ( 𝔼 ⁡ ( ∑ j ≠ 1 ∑ k = 1 n 1 ​ j Y 1 ​ j ​ k ) , … , 𝔼 ⁡ ( ∑ j ≠ K ∑ k = 1 n K ​ j Y K ​ j ​ k ) ) ⊤ \displaystyle(\mathbb{E}(S_{1}),\ldots,\mathbb{E}(S_{K}))^{\top}=(\mathbb{E}(\sum_{j\neq 1}\sum_{k=1}^{n_{1j}}Y_{1jk}),\ldots,\mathbb{E}(\sum_{j\neq K}\sum_{k=1}^{n_{Kj}}Y_{Kjk}))^{\top} = \displaystyle= ( ∑ j ≠ 1 n 1 ​ j ​ ( μ 1 − μ j ) , … , ∑ j ≠ K n K ​ j ​ ( μ K − μ j ) ) ⊤ = 𝐍 ⁡ 𝝁 \displaystyle(\sum_{j\neq 1}n_{1j}\left(\mu_{1}-\mu_{j}\right),\ldots,\sum_{j\neq K}n_{Kj}\left(\mu_{K}-\mu_{j}\right))^{\top}=\bN\bmu so 𝔼 ⁡ ( 𝝁 ^ ) = 𝐍 + ⁡ 𝐍 ​ 𝝁 \mathbb{E}(\widehat{\bmu})=\bN^{+}\boldsymbol{N\mu} . Hence 𝝁 ^ \widehat{\bmu} is unbiased. (7) It is clear from ( 7 ) that the precision of the LSE is a function of 𝐍 + \bN^{+} and therefore depends on the properties of the comparison graph a fact further reinforced by observing that the mean squared error of 𝝁 ^ n \widehat{\bmu}_{n} is 𝔼 ⁡ ( ‖ 𝝁 ^ n − 𝝁 ‖ 2 2 ) \displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\left\|\widehat{\bmu}_{n}-\bmu\right\|_{2}^{2}) = \displaystyle= 𝔼 ⁡ ( ‖ 𝐍 + ​ 𝐒 − 𝐍 + ⁡ 𝐍 ​ 𝝁 ‖ 2 2 ) = 𝔼 ⁡ ( ‖ 𝐍 + ⁡ ( 𝐒 − 𝐍 ​ 𝝁 ) ‖ 2 2 ) \displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\left\|\bN^{+}\bS-\bN^{+}\boldsymbol{N\mu}\right\|_{2}^{2})=\mathbb{E(}\left\|\bN^{+}(\bS-\boldsymbol{N\mu})\right\|_{2}^{2}) = \displaystyle= 𝔼 ⁡ ( ( 𝐒 − 𝐍 ​ 𝝁 ) ⊤ ​ ( 𝐍 + ⁡ 𝐍 + ) ​ ( 𝐒 − 𝐍 ​ 𝝁 ) ) = trace ⁡ ( 𝐍 + ⁡ 𝐍 + ⁡ ( σ 2 ​ 𝐍 ) ) = σ 2 ​ trace ​ ( 𝐍 + ) . \displaystyle\mathbb{E(}(\bS-\boldsymbol{N\mu})^{\top}(\bN^{+}\bN^{+})(\bS-\boldsymbol{N\mu}))=\mathrm{trace}(\bN^{+}\bN^{+}(\sigma^{2}\bN))=\sigma^{2}\mathrm{trace}(\bN^{+}). The following example shows that even when the total number of paired comparisons is fixed, the structure of the graph 𝒢 \mathcal{G} has a powerful effect on the precision of the estimators. Example 2.1 We evaluate the precision of the LSE on several types of graphs. These include the complete, cycle, path, star, wheel and (knockout) tournament graphs as displayed in Figure 1 . In the context of the tournament graph we assume, without any loss of generality, that the item with the smaller index moves up the tournament graph. Figure 1 Comes Here In a complete graph with K K items there are K ⁡ ( K − 1 ) / 2 K(K-1)/2 paired comparisons whereas in a path graph there are only K − 1 K-1 paired comparisons. Therefore a meaningful comparison among the graphs requires that all Laplacians be scaled so that the total number of paired comparisons, which is equal to trace ⁡ ( 𝐍 ) / 2 \rm{trace}(\bN)/2 , is common to all graphs. For convenience we choose to scale up to the complete graph. Since, the standard tournament graph has 2 m 2^{m} vertices, where m ∈ ℕ m\in\mathbb{N} , we choose K ∈ { 4 , 8 , 16 , 32 , 64 , 128 , 256 , 512 } K\in\{4,8,16,32,64,128,256,512\} . We are interested in the overall precision of 𝛍 ^ \widehat{\bmu} which can be assessed, cf., Atkinson et al. (2007), by various functions of the (positive) eigenvalues of 𝐍 + \bN^{+} such as their sum, product, largest and smallest value. In Figure 2(a) and 2(b) , we display the sum of the eigenvalues and the maximum eigenvalues of the complete, cycle, path, star, wheel and (knockout) tournament graphs as a function of the number of items we are comparing. Larger values indicate higher variability. Figure 2 Comes Here Each type of graph is associated with a line in Figures 2(a) and 2(b) . The top line corresponds to the path graph on which the estimators are most variable and the bottom line is associated with the complete graph which generates the most precise estimators; the performances of (the scaled) star, wheel and knockout graphs are close to those of the complete graph. Thus, graphs with high connectivity (Cvetković et al. 2009) result in more precise estimators. Among the displayed graphs, the path graph shows the fastest increase in variability as a function of K K . This is due to its low connectivity. As an example consider comparing the difference between the first and last items in a path graph. Obviously these items are not directly compared. In fact, 𝕍 ​ a ​ r ​ ( μ ^ 1 − μ ^ K ) = 𝕍 ​ a ​ r ​ ( ( μ ^ 1 − μ ^ 2 ) + ( μ ^ 2 − μ ^ 3 ) + ⋯ + ( μ ^ K − 1 − μ ^ K ) ) , \mathbb{V}ar(\widehat{\mu}_{1}-\widehat{\mu}_{K})=\mathbb{V}ar((\widehat{\mu}_{1}-\widehat{\mu}_{2})+(\widehat{\mu}_{2}-\widehat{\mu}_{3})+\cdots+(\widehat{\mu}_{K-1}-\widehat{\mu}_{K})), i.e., the variance of their difference is compounded over a long path. In high connectivity graphs the paths between any two items is short and therefore comparisons among items are more precise. The performance of the knockout tournament graph is better than the path and cycle graph. It is also interesting to note that all lines in Figures 2(a) and 2(b) are increasing except for the line associated with the complete graph. Finally, in symmetric graphs in which all vertices have the same degree, the components of 𝛍 \bmu are estimated with the same variance, e.g., complete graph, cycle graph. If the vertices of the graph have different degrees then the components of 𝛍 \bmu are estimated with different variances. The merits of central items (cf., Cvetković et al. 2009) are also estimated with more precision. For example, the merit of the item in the center of the path graph is estimated more precisely than items at the periphery of the graph. Similarly for the central item of the star graph and the wheel graph. We introduce further notations. The graph 𝒢 2 \mathcal{G}_{2} is a subgraph of the graph 𝒢 1 \mathcal{G}_{1} , denoted 𝒢 1 ⊇ 𝒢 2 \mathcal{G}_{1}\supseteq\mathcal{G}_{2} , if 𝒱 1 ⊇ 𝒱 2 \mathcal{V}_{1}\supseteq\mathcal{V}_{2} and ℰ 1 ⊇ ℰ 2 \mathcal{E}_{1}\supseteq\mathcal{E}_{2} . The latter notion extends to PCG where ( 𝒢 1 , 𝒴 1 ) ⊇ ( 𝒢 2 , 𝒴 2 ) (\mathcal{G}_{1},\mathcal{Y}_{1})\supseteq(\mathcal{G}_{2},\mathcal{Y}_{2}) whenever 𝒢 1 ⊇ 𝒢 2 \mathcal{G}_{1}\supseteq\mathcal{G}_{2} and 𝒴 1 ⊇ 𝒴 2 \mathcal{Y}_{1}\supseteq\mathcal{Y}_{2} , i.e., if Y i ​ j ​ k ∈ 𝒴 2 Y_{ijk}\in\mathcal{Y}_{2} then Y i ​ j ​ k ∈ 𝒴 1 Y_{ijk}\in\mathcal{Y}_{1} . A matrix 𝐕 1 \bV_{1} is said to be smaller than a matrix 𝐕 2 \bV_{2} , in the Loewner order, if 𝐕 2 − 𝐕 1 \bV_{2}-\bV_{1} is non–negative definite. This relationship is denoted by 𝐕 1 ⪯ 𝐕 2 \bV_{1}\preceq\bV_{2} . For more on the Loewner order see Pukelsheim (2006). In particular if 𝐕 1 \bV_{1} and 𝐕 2 \bV_{2} are the variances of two (asymptotically) unbiased estimators (of the same quantity) then 𝐕 1 ⪯ 𝐕 2 \bV_{1}\preceq\bV_{2} implies that the estimator associated with 𝐕 1 \bV_{1} is more efficient than the estimator associated with 𝐕 2 \bV_{2} . This means, as an example, that the volume of the confidence ellipsoid associated with 𝐕 1 \bV_{1} is smaller than the volume of the confidence ellipsoid associated with 𝐕 2 \bV_{2} . Proposition 2.2 Let ( 𝒢 1 , 𝒴 1 ) (\mathcal{G}_{1},\mathcal{Y}_{1}) and ( 𝒢 2 , 𝒴 2 ) (\mathcal{G}_{2},\mathcal{Y}_{2}) be comparison graphs on the same set of vertices with unique LSEs 𝛍 ^ 1 \widehat{\bmu}_{1} and 𝛍 ^ 2 \widehat{\bmu}_{2} whose variances are 𝐕 1 \bV_{1} and 𝐕 2 \bV_{2} respectively. If ( 𝒢 1 , 𝒴 1 ) ⊇ ( 𝒢 2 , 𝒴 2 ) (\mathcal{G}_{1},\mathcal{Y}_{1})\supseteq(\mathcal{G}_{2},\mathcal{Y}_{2}) then 𝐕 1 ⪯ 𝐕 2 . \bV_{1}\preceq\bV_{2}. (8) Moreover, for any continuously differentiable function 𝚽 ⁡ ( 𝛍 ) \boldsymbol{\Phi}(\bmu) we have 𝐕 1 𝚽 ⪯ 𝐕 2 𝚽 \bV_{1}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}}\preceq\bV_{2}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}} where 𝐕 1 𝚽 \bV_{1}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}} and 𝐕 2 𝚽 \bV_{2}^{\boldsymbol{\Phi}} are the asymptotic variances of 𝚽 ⁡ ( 𝛍 ^ 1 ) \boldsymbol{\Phi}(\widehat{\bmu}_{1}) and 𝚽 ⁡ ( 𝛍 ^ 2 ) \boldsymbol{\Phi}(\widehat{\bmu}_{2}) respectively. Proposition 2.2 shows that the variance of the LSE or any function thereof decreases when the number of paired comparisons on each edge increases. 3 Large sample theory The asymptotic theory for the LSE, denoted henceforth by 𝝁 ^ n \widehat{\bmu}_{n} to emphasize its dependence on the total number of paired comparisons, requires some additional graph–based notions. A graph 𝒯 \mathcal{T} is called a tree if any two distinct vertices in 𝒯 \mathcal{T} are joined by a unique path. If the tree 𝒯 \mathcal{T} is a subgraph of 𝒢 \mathcal{G} and connects all vertices in 𝒢 \mathcal{G} then it is called a spanning tree. Clearly, if 𝒢 \mathcal{G} is connected then a spanning tree 𝒯 \mathcal{T} exists (Gould, 2012). Condition 3.1 There exists a spanning tree 𝒯 ⊆ 𝒢 \mathcal{T}\subseteq\mathcal{G} such that min ⁡ { n i ​ j : ( i , j ) ∈ 𝒯 } → ∞ \min\{n_{ij}:\left(i,j\right)\in\mathcal{T}\}\rightarrow\infty as n → ∞ n\rightarrow\infty . Condition 3.1 ensures that the minimum number of comparisons along some spanning tree increases to infinity. More concretely, for any pair i , j ∈ 𝒱 i,\,j\in\mathcal{V} , either n i ​ j → ∞ n_{ij}\to\infty or there exists a path v 1 , v 2 , … , v l ∈ 𝒱 v_{1},v_{2},\ldots,v_{l}\in\mathcal{V} such that min ⁡ { n i ​ v 1 , n v 1 ​ v 2 , n v 2 ​ v 3 , … , n v l − 1 ​ v l , n v l ​ j } → ∞ \min\{n_{iv_{1}},n_{v_{1}v_{2}},n_{v_{2}v_{3}},\ldots,n_{v_{l-1}v_{l}},n_{v_{l}j}\}\to\infty . That is, all pairs of items are compared infinitely many times either directly or indirectly. The rate at which n i ​ j n_{ij} where ( i , j ) ∈ 𝒯 (i,j)\in\mathcal{T} grows to infinity is left unspecified; in fact the rate may be edge specific. There may be many spanning trees on which Condition 3.1 holds. We define m = max 𝒯 ⊂ 𝒢 ⁡ min ⁡ { n i ​ j : ( i , j ) ∈ 𝒯 } . \displaystyle m=\displaystyle{\max_{\mathcal{T}\subset\mathcal{G}}}\min\{n_{ij}:(i,j)\in\mathcal{T}\}. (9) and denote a tree on which ( 9 ) is attained by 𝒯 m \mathcal{T}_{m} . Thus, 𝒯 m \mathcal{T}_{m} is a tree on which the minimum number of paired comparisons grows the fastest. Theorem 3.1 If Conditions 2.1 and 3.1 hold then 𝕍 ​ a ​ r ​ ( 𝛍 ^ n ) → 𝐎 \mathbb{V}ar(\widehat{\bmu}_{n})\rightarrow\boldsymbol{O} , where 𝐎 \boldsymbol{O} is the K × K {K\times K} matrix of zeros and consequently 𝛍 ^ n → 𝛍 \widehat{\bmu}_{n}\rightarrow\bmu in probability. Moreover for each i i , 𝕍 ​ a ​ r ​ ( μ ^ i , n ) = O ⁡ ( 1 / m ) . \mathbb{V}ar(\widehat{\mu}_{i,n})=O(1/m). (10) Finally, if Condition 3.1 does not hold then 𝕍 ​ a ​ r ​ ( 𝛍 ^ n ) ↛ 𝐎 \mathbb{V}ar(\widehat{\bmu}_{n})\not\to\boldsymbol{O} . Theorem 3.1 establishes that the LSE is consistent provided Condition 3.1 holds. In the proof of Theorem 3.1 it is shown that Condition 3.1 is necessary and sufficient for the second smallest eigenvalue of 𝐍 \bN , known as the algebraic connectivity (Cvetkovic et al. 2009) and denoted by λ 2 = λ 2 ​ ( 𝐍 ) \lambda_{2}=\lambda_{2}(\bN) , to converge to ∞ \infty . Using graph decomposition arguments, and Weyl’s inequality (Horn and Johnson, 2007) it can be shown that the latter implies that 𝐍 + → 𝐎 \bN^{+}\to\boldsymbol{O} , the zero matrix, so 𝝁 ^ n \widehat{\bmu}_{n} is consistent. Moreover, by the spectral decomposition 𝐍 + = O ⁡ ( 1 / λ 2 ) \bN^{+}=O(1/\lambda_{2}) , element–wise. In addition, it is shown that λ 2 = O ⁡ ( m ) . \lambda_{2}=O(m). (11) Thus, the variance of any merit estimator is of order 1 / m 1/m , where m m is defined in ( 9 ), and is not directly related to the sample size n i n_{i} as in the usual K K sample case. Note that Condition 3.1 implies that n i → ∞ n_{i}\to\infty for all i ∈ 𝒱 i\in\mathcal{V} ; the reverse implication is not necessarily true. For example consider the graph 𝒢 \mathcal{G} with 𝒱 = { 1 , 2 , 3 , 4 } \mathcal{V}=\{1,2,3,4\} and ℰ = { ( 1 , 2 ) , ( 2 , 3 ) , ( 3 , 4 ) } \mathcal{E}=\{(1,2),(2,3),(3,4)\} where k = n 12 = n 34 k=n_{12}=n_{34} and n 23 = 1 n_{23}=1 . Clearly n i → ∞ n_{i}\to\infty for all i i as k → ∞ k\to\infty but Condition 3.1 does not hold. Moreover, for this graph λ 2 = k + 1 − k 2 + 1 ≤ 1 \lambda_{2}=k+1-\sqrt{k^{2}+1}\leq 1 for all k ∈ ℕ k\in\mathbb{N} . Therefore 𝐍 + ↛ 𝐎 \bN^{+}\not\to\boldsymbol{O} and the LSE is not consistent. It is also worthwhile noting that in general O ⁡ ( m ) &lt; O ⁡ ( min ⁡ { n 1 , … , n K } ) O(m)&lt;O(\min\{n_{1},\ldots,n_{K}\}) , however there are situations in which either m = O ⁡ ( min ⁡ { n 1 , … , n K } ) m=O(\min\{n_{1},\ldots,n_{K}\}) or m = O ⁡ ( n ) m=O(n) . Remark 3.1 As noted by a referee we use the O ⁡ ( ⋅ ) O(\cdot) notation correctly, albeit, somewhat casually. For example, in Equation ( 11 ) we write λ 2 = O ⁡ ( m ) \lambda_{2}=O(m) . However, in the proof of Theorem 3.1 it is shown that λ 2 / m → c \lambda_{2}/m\to c for some constant c c which depends on the topology of the graph. Thus, it would have been more precise to write λ 2 = Θ ⁡ ( m ) \lambda_{2}=\Theta(m) as common in the literature in computer science, e.g., Cormen et al. (2022). Nevertheless, we will adhere to the less precise O ⁡ ( ⋅ ) O(\cdot) notation as common in the statistical literature. Next, strong consistency without a variance is established. Theorem 3.2 If the errors in ( 1 ) are IID with mean zero then 𝛍 ^ n → 𝛍 \widehat{\bmu}_{n}\rightarrow\bmu with probability one if and only if Condition 3.1 holds. Thus, provided the errors have mean zero, the merits converge to their true values with probability one. The rate of convergence is explored by simulation in Section 6 . The proof of Theorem 3.2 is rather involved. In addition to the usual probabilistic arguments the proof relies on specialized results in linear algebra (Satorra and Neudecker, 2015) and graph theory. In particular we use the all minors matrix tree theorem (Chen 1976, Chaiken, 1982) and various properties of Laplacians (Chebotarev and Shamis 1998, and Bapat 2013). The large sampling distribution of the LSE is explored under two different regimes. Condition 3.2 There exists a spanning tree 𝒯 ⊂ 𝒢 \mathcal{T}\subset\mathcal{G} such that min ⁡ { n i ​ j : ( i , j ) ∈ 𝒯 } / n → c ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ) ​ as ​ n → ∞ . \min\{n_{ij}:(i,j)\in\mathcal{T}\}/n\to c\in(0,\infty)\text{ as }n\to\infty. It is clear that Condition 3.2 is stronger than Condition 3.1 . Condition 3.2 implies that n i ​ j / n l ​ k → c i ​ j ​ l ​ k ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ) n_{ij}/n_{lk}\rightarrow c_{ijlk}\in(0,\infty) for all pairs ( i , j ) (i,j) and ( l , k ) (l,k) in 𝒯 \mathcal{T} . It can be further shown that Condition 3.2 implies that for any two items n i / n j → c i ​ j ∈ ( 0 , ∞ ) n_{i}/n_{j}\rightarrow c_{ij}\in(0,\infty) , i.e., the total number of paired comparisons for all items grows at the same rate. Formally this means that n i ​ j = O ⁡ ( n ) n_{ij}=O(n) for all ( i , j ) ∈ 𝒯 (i,j)\in\mathcal{T} and n i = O ⁡ ( n ) n_{i}=O(n) for all i ∈ 𝒱 i\in\mathcal{V} . However, n i = O ⁡ ( n ) n_{i}=O(n) for all i ∈ 𝒱 i\in\mathcal{V} does not imply that Condition 3.2 holds. For example, consider a path graph with four vertices for which n 12 = n 34 = m 2 n_{12}=n_{34}=m^{2} and n 23 = m n_{23}=m . It is easy to verify that n i = O ⁡ ( m 2 ) n_{i}=O(m^{2}) whereas min ⁡ n i ​ j / n → 0 \min{n_{ij}}/n\to 0 . Condition 3.2 results in well–behaved variance matrices for the LSE. Lemma 3.1 If Condition 3.2 holds then 𝚯 = lim n 𝐍 / n \boldsymbol{\Theta}=\lim_{n}\bN/n exists and rank ⁡ ( 𝚯 ) = K − 1 \mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol{\Theta})=K-1 . Moreover n ​ 𝐍 + → 𝚯 + n\bN^{+}\to{\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{+}} as n → ∞ n\to\infty where all diagonal elements of 𝚯 + \boldsymbol{\Theta}^{+} are positive and rank ⁡ ( 𝚯 + ) = K − 1 \mathrm{rank}(\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{+})=K-1 . We are now ready for our first limit law. Theorem 3.3 Suppose that Conditions 2.1 and 3.2 hold. Then, n ​ ( 𝛍 ^ n − 𝛍 ) ⇒ 𝒩 K ​ ( 𝟎 , σ 2 ​ 𝚯 + ) \sqrt{n}\left(\widehat{\bmu}_{n}-\bmu\right)\Rightarrow\mathcal{N}_{K}(\boldsymbol{0},\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{+}) . We conclude that under Condition 3.2 the centered LSE, scaled by n \sqrt{n} , approximately follows a normal distribution in large samples. The asymptotic variance in Theorem 3.3 is determined only by the limit of the graph Laplacian, i.e., by connectivity properties of 𝒢 \mathcal{G} . In particular the limiting variance depends only on only those ( i , j ) ∈ ℰ (i,j)\in\mathcal{E} for which n i ​ j = O ⁡ ( n ) n_{ij}=O(n) , all others are awash. Remark 3.2 Following a comment by a referee we note that using Proposition 2.1 and Theorem 3.3 we deduce that for any proper constraint vector 𝐮 \boldsymbol{u} we have n ​ ( 𝛍 ^ n ​ ( 𝐮 ) − 𝛍 ⁡ ( 𝐮 ) ) ⇒ 𝒩 K ​ ( 𝟎 , σ 2 ​ 𝐂 𝐮 ​ 𝚯 + ​ 𝐂 𝐮 ⊤ ) \sqrt{n}(\widehat{\bmu}_{n}(\boldsymbol{u})-\bmu(\boldsymbol{u}))\Rightarrow\mathcal{N}_{K}(\boldsymbol{0},\sigma^{2}\boldsymbol{C}_{\boldsymbol{u}}\boldsymbol{\Theta}^{+}\boldsymbol{C}_{\boldsymbol{u}}^{\top}) , where 𝛍 ⁡ ( 𝐮 ) = 𝐂 𝐮 ​ 𝛍 \bmu(\boldsymbol{u})=\bC_{\bu}\bmu and 𝛍 ∈ im ⁡ ( 𝐍 ) \bmu\in\rm{im}(\bN) . Further note that Condition 3.2 , which implies Lemma 3.1 , is necessary for Theorem 3.3 as exemplified below. Example 3.1 Consider the tree–structured comparison graph with K = 3 K=3 , n 12 = m n_{12}=m and n 23 = m 2 n_{23}=m^{2} so n = m + m 2 n=m+m^{2} . Note that if m → ∞ m\to\infty Condition 3.1 holds but condition 3.2 does not.
