The result for 𝑪 ​ 𝐍 + ​ 𝐂 ⊤ ​ 𝐃 \boldsymbol{C}\bN^{+}\boldsymbol{C}^{\top}\boldsymbol{D} is now straihtforward. Let 𝔗 {\frakfamily T} be the set of all spanning trees and 𝔗 i ​ j {\frakfamily T}_{ij} the set of spanning trees containing edge ( i , j ) (i,j) . For any 𝒯 ∈ 𝔗 \mathcal{T}\in{\frakfamily T} let w ⁡ ( 𝒯 ) = ∏ ( i , j ) ∈ 𝒯 n i ​ j w(\mathcal{T})=\prod_{(i,j)\in\mathcal{T}}n_{ij} be its weight. Then we have the following: Lemma A.5 All entries of 𝐂 ​ 𝐍 + ​ 𝐂 T ​ 𝐃 \boldsymbol{C}\bN^{+}\boldsymbol{C}^{T}\boldsymbol{D} have absolute value bounded by one. Proof. By the all minors matrix tree theorem (Chen, 1976, Chaiken, 1982) we get that | 𝐍 1 1 | = ∑ 𝒯 ∈ 𝔗 w ⁡ ( 𝒯 ) |\bN_{1}^{1}|=\sum_{\mathcal{T}\in{\frakfamily T}}w(\mathcal{T}) and n i ​ j ​ | 𝐍 k ​ ℓ ij | = ∑ 𝒯 ∈ C ij s 𝒯 , ij ​ w ​ ( 𝒯 ) n_{ij}|\bN_{k\ell}^{ij}|=\sum_{\mathcal{T}\in C_{ij}}s_{\mathcal{T},ij}w(\mathcal{T}) , where ℭ i ​ j ⊆ 𝔗 i ​ j {\frakfamily C}_{ij}\subseteq{\frakfamily T}_{ij} is described in (Chaiken 1982) and s 𝒯 , i ​ j s_{\mathcal{T},ij} is some sign. Thus, | ( − 1 ) k + ℓ + i + j ​ n i ​ j ​ | 𝐍 k ​ ℓ ij | / | 𝐍 1 1 | | ⩽ ∑ 𝒯 ∈ C ij w ⁡ ( 𝒯 ) / ∑ 𝒯 ∈ 𝔗 w ⁡ ( 𝒯 ) ⩽ 1 . \big|(-1)^{k+\ell+i+j}n_{ij}|\bN_{k\ell}^{ij}|/|\bN_{1}^{1}|\big|\leqslant\sum_{\mathcal{T}\in C_{ij}}w(\mathcal{T})/\sum_{\mathcal{T}\in{\frakfamily T}}w(\mathcal{T})\leqslant 1. Assume now that n = ∑ i , j n i , j → ∞ n=\sum_{i,j}n_{i,j}\to\infty and Condition 3.1 holds. Note that there may exist n i ​ j n_{ij} ’s which don’t go to infinity and even if they do they may go to infinity at different rates. For a spanning tree 𝒯 \mathcal{T} let r 𝒯 ​ ( n ) r_{\mathcal{T}}(n) be the rate at which w ⁡ ( 𝒯 ) w(\mathcal{T}) goes to infinity with r 𝒯 ​ ( n ) = 1 r_{\mathcal{T}}(n)=1 if w ⁡ ( 𝒯 ) ↛ ∞ w(\mathcal{T})\nrightarrow\infty . Let r ~ ​ ( n ) \tilde{r}(n) be the maximum rate over 𝒯 ∈ 𝔗 \mathcal{T}\in{\frakfamily T} . Let 𝔗 ~ = { 𝒯 ∈ 𝔗 : w ⁡ ( 𝒯 ) = O ⁡ ( r ~ ​ ( n ) ) } \tilde{{\frakfamily T}}=\{\mathcal{T}\in{\frakfamily T}:w(\mathcal{T})=O(\tilde{r}(n))\} and ℰ ~ \tilde{\mathcal{E}} be the set of edges ( i , j ) (i,j) which belong to some 𝒯 ∈ 𝔗 ~ \mathcal{T}\in\tilde{{\frakfamily T}} . Let 𝑫 ~ = diag ⁡ ( n ~ 12 , … , n ~ 1 ​ K , n ~ 23 , … , n ~ 2 ​ K , … , n ~ K − 1 , K ) \tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}=\mathrm{diag}(\tilde{n}_{12},\ldots,\tilde{n}_{1K},\tilde{n}_{23},\ldots,\tilde{n}_{2K},\ldots,\tilde{n}_{K-1,K}) , where n ~ i ​ j = n i ​ j \tilde{n}_{ij}=n_{ij} if ( i , j ) ∈ ℰ ~ (i,j)\in\tilde{\mathcal{E}} and zero otherwise. Set finally 𝐍 ~ = 𝑪 T ​ 𝑫 ~ ​ 𝑪 \tilde{\bN}=\boldsymbol{C}^{T}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}\boldsymbol{C} and let 𝐍 ~ + \tilde{\bN}^{+} be its Moore-Penrose inverse. Note that 𝐍 ~ \tilde{\bN} is also the Laplacian of a multigraph. Then, we have the following. Lemma A.6 lim n ↑ ∞ ( 𝑪 ​ 𝐍 + ​ 𝐂 T ​ 𝐃 − 𝐂 ​ 𝐍 ~ + ​ 𝐂 T ​ 𝐃 ~ ) = 𝐎 \lim_{n\uparrow\infty}(\boldsymbol{C}\bN^{+}\boldsymbol{C}^{T}\boldsymbol{D}-\boldsymbol{C}\tilde{\bN}^{+}\boldsymbol{C}^{T}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}})=\boldsymbol{O} . Proof. By Lemma A.4 , the ( k , ℓ , i , j ) (k,\ell;i,j) entries of 𝑪 ​ 𝐍 + ​ 𝐂 ⊤ ​ 𝐃 \boldsymbol{C}\bN^{+}\boldsymbol{C}^{\top}\boldsymbol{D} and 𝑪 ​ 𝐍 ~ + ​ 𝑪 ⊤ ​ 𝑫 ~ \boldsymbol{C}\tilde{\bN}^{+}\boldsymbol{C}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}} are ( − 1 ) k + ℓ + i + j ​ n i ​ j (-1)^{k+\ell+i+j}n_{ij} | 𝐍 k ​ ℓ ij | / | 𝐍 1 1 | |\bN_{k\ell}^{ij}|/|\bN_{1}^{1}| and ( − 1 ) k + ℓ + i + j ​ n ~ i ​ j ​ | 𝐍 ~ k ​ ℓ i ​ j | / | 𝐍 ~ 1 1 | (-1)^{k+\ell+i+j}\tilde{n}_{ij}|\tilde{\bN}_{k\ell}^{ij}|/|\tilde{\bN}_{1}^{1}| , respectively. By applying once more the all minors matrix tree theorem we get | 𝐍 ~ 1 1 | = ∑ 𝒯 ∈ 𝔗 ~ w ⁡ ( 𝒯 ) |\tilde{\bN}_{1}^{1}|=\sum_{\mathcal{T}\in\tilde{{\frakfamily T}}}w(\mathcal{T}) and n ~ i ​ j ​ | 𝐍 ~ k ​ ℓ i ​ j | = ∑ 𝒯 ∈ C i ​ j ∩ 𝔗 ~ s 𝒯 , i ​ j ​ w ​ ( 𝒯 ) \tilde{n}_{ij}|\tilde{\bN}_{k\ell}^{ij}|=\sum_{\mathcal{T}\in C_{ij}\cap\tilde{{\frakfamily T}}}s_{\mathcal{T},ij}w(\mathcal{T}) by construction. Since r 𝒯 ​ ( n ) = o ⁡ ( r ~ ​ ( n ) ) r_{\mathcal{T}}(n)=o(\tilde{r}(n)) for 𝒯 ∉ 𝔗 ~ \mathcal{T}\notin\tilde{{\frakfamily T}} we may write | 𝐍 1 1 | = | 𝐍 ~ 1 1 | + o ⁡ ( r ~ ​ ( n ) ) |\bN_{1}^{1}|=|\tilde{\bN}_{1}^{1}|+o(\tilde{r}(n)) and n i ​ j ​ | 𝐍 k ​ ℓ ij | = n ~ ij ​ | 𝐍 ~ k ​ ℓ ij | + o ⁡ ( r ~ ​ ( n ) ) n_{ij}|\bN_{k\ell}^{ij}|=\tilde{n}_{ij}|\tilde{\boldsymbol{N}}_{k\ell}^{ij}|+o(\tilde{r}(n)) . Thus, n i ​ j ​ | 𝐍 k ​ ℓ ij | | 𝐍 1 1 | − n ~ i ​ j ​ | 𝐍 ~ k ​ ℓ i ​ j | | 𝐍 ~ 1 1 | = n ~ i ​ j ​ | 𝐍 ~ k ​ ℓ i ​ j | + o ⁡ ( r ~ ​ ( n ) ) | 𝐍 ~ 1 1 | + o ⁡ ( r ~ ​ ( n ) ) − n ~ i ​ j ​ | 𝐍 ~ k ​ ℓ i ​ j | | 𝐍 ~ 1 1 | = | 𝐍 ~ 1 1 | ​ o ​ ( r ~ ​ ( n ) ) − n ~ i ​ j ​ | 𝐍 ~ k ​ ℓ i ​ j | ​ o ​ ( r ~ ​ ( n ) ) | 𝐍 ~ 1 1 | ​ { | 𝐍 ~ 1 1 | + o ⁡ ( r ~ ​ ( n ) ) } \frac{n_{ij}|\bN_{k\ell}^{ij}|}{|\bN_{1}^{1}|}-\frac{\tilde{n}_{ij}|\tilde{\bN}_{k\ell}^{ij}|}{|\tilde{\bN}_{1}^{1}|}=\frac{\tilde{n}_{ij}|\tilde{\bN}_{k\ell}^{ij}|+o(\tilde{r}(n))}{|\tilde{\bN}_{1}^{1}|+o(\tilde{r}(n))}-\frac{\tilde{n}_{ij}|\tilde{\bN}_{k\ell}^{ij}|}{|\tilde{\bN}_{1}^{1}|}=\frac{|\tilde{\bN}_{1}^{1}|o(\tilde{r}(n))-\tilde{n}_{ij}|\tilde{\bN}_{k\ell}^{ij}|o(\tilde{r}(n))}{|\tilde{\bN}_{1}^{1}|\{|\tilde{\bN}_{1}^{1}|+o(\tilde{r}(n))\}} which converges to zero since the numerator is o ⁡ ( r ~ ​ ( n ) 2 ) o(\tilde{r}(n)^{2}) and the denominator O ⁡ ( r ~ ​ ( n ) 2 ) O(\tilde{r}(n)^{2}) . Proof of Theorem 3.2 : Proof. Let 𝜹 ^ = 𝑪 ​ 𝝁 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\delta}}=\boldsymbol{C}\widehat{\bmu} . By applying Lemma A.1 (c) with 𝝁 ^ \widehat{\bmu} and 𝜹 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\delta}} in the place of 𝝁 \bmu and 𝜹 \boldsymbol{\delta} we conclude that 𝝁 ^ \widehat{\bmu} is consistent for 𝝁 \bmu if and only if 𝜹 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\delta}} is consistent for 𝜹 \boldsymbol{\delta} . By the strong law of large numbers, if n i ​ j → ∞ n_{ij}\rightarrow\infty (at whatever rate) then Y ¯ i ​ j → μ i − μ j \bar{Y}_{ij}\rightarrow\mu_{i}-\mu_{j} almost surely. Therefore, we may write 𝐒 ¯ ​ ⟶ a . s . ​ 𝜹 ~ = ( δ ~ 11 , … , δ ~ 1 ​ K , δ ~ 23 , … , δ ~ 2 ​ K , … , δ ~ K − 1 , K ) \bar{\bS}\overset{a.s.}{\longrightarrow}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\delta}}=(\tilde{\delta}_{11},\ldots,\tilde{\delta}_{1K},\tilde{\delta}_{23},\ldots,\tilde{\delta}_{2K},\ldots,\tilde{\delta}_{K-1,K}) where δ ~ i ​ j = μ i − μ j \tilde{\delta}_{ij}=\mu_{i}-\mu_{j} or Y ¯ i ​ j \bar{Y}_{ij} according to if n i ​ j → ∞ n_{ij}\rightarrow\infty or not. Suppose first that Condition 3.1 is satisfied. By applying Lemma A.1 with 𝐍 ~ \tilde{\bN} instead of 𝐍 \bN we get 𝑪 ​ 𝐍 ~ + ​ 𝑪 ⊤ ​ 𝑫 ~ ​ 𝜹 ~ = 𝜹 ~ \boldsymbol{C}\tilde{\bN}^{+}\boldsymbol{C}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\delta}}=\tilde{\boldsymbol{\delta}} . However, if ( i , j ) ∉ ℰ ~ (i,j)\notin\tilde{\mathcal{E}} then the ( i , j ) (i,j) th column of 𝑪 ​ 𝐍 ~ + ​ 𝑪 ⊤ ​ 𝑫 ~ \boldsymbol{C}\tilde{\bN}^{+}\boldsymbol{C}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}} contains only zeros. Since 𝜹 ~ i ​ j = μ i − μ j \tilde{\boldsymbol{\delta}}_{ij}=\mu_{i}-\mu_{j} for all ( i , j ) ∈ ℰ ~ (i,j)\in\tilde{\mathcal{E}} we conclude that 𝑪 ​ 𝐍 ~ + ​ 𝑪 ⊤ ​ 𝑫 ~ ​ 𝜹 ~ = 𝑪 ​ 𝐍 ~ + ​ 𝑪 ⊤ ​ 𝑫 ~ ​ 𝜹 = 𝜹 \boldsymbol{C}\tilde{\bN}^{+}\boldsymbol{C}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\delta}}=\boldsymbol{C}\tilde{\bN}^{+}\boldsymbol{C}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}\boldsymbol{\delta}=\boldsymbol{\delta} . Thus, 𝜹 ^ = \displaystyle\widehat{\boldsymbol{\delta}}= 𝑪 ​ 𝐍 + ​ 𝐂 ⊤ ​ 𝐃 ​ 𝐒 ¯ \displaystyle\boldsymbol{C}\bN^{+}\boldsymbol{C}^{\top}\boldsymbol{D}\bar{\bS} = \displaystyle= ( 𝑪 ​ 𝐍 + ​ 𝐂 ⊤ ​ 𝐃 − 𝐂 ​ 𝐍 ~ + ​ 𝐂 ⊤ ​ 𝐃 ~ ) ​ 𝐒 ¯ + 𝐂 ​ 𝐍 ~ + ​ 𝐂 ⊤ ​ 𝐃 ~ ​ ( 𝐒 ¯ − 𝜹 ~ ) + 𝐂 ​ 𝐍 ~ + ​ 𝐂 ⊤ ​ 𝐃 ~ ​ 𝜹 ~ \displaystyle(\boldsymbol{C}\bN^{+}\boldsymbol{C}^{\top}\boldsymbol{D}-\boldsymbol{C}\tilde{\bN}^{+}\boldsymbol{C}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}})\bar{\bS}+\boldsymbol{C}\tilde{\bN}^{+}\boldsymbol{C}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}(\bar{\bS}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{\delta}})+\boldsymbol{C}\tilde{\bN}^{+}\boldsymbol{C}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}\tilde{\boldsymbol{\delta}} = \displaystyle= ( 𝑪 ​ 𝐍 + ​ 𝐂 ⊤ ​ 𝐃 − 𝐂 ​ 𝐍 ~ + ​ 𝐂 ⊤ ​ 𝐃 ~ ) ​ 𝐒 ¯ + 𝐂 ​ 𝐍 ~ + ​ 𝐂 ⊤ ​ 𝐃 ~ ​ ( 𝐒 ¯ − 𝜹 ~ ) + 𝜹 ​ ⟶ a . s . ​ 𝜹 , \displaystyle(\boldsymbol{C}\bN^{+}\boldsymbol{C}^{\top}\boldsymbol{D}-\boldsymbol{C}\tilde{\bN}^{+}\boldsymbol{C}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}})\bar{\bS}+\boldsymbol{C}\tilde{\bN}^{+}\boldsymbol{C}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}}(\bar{\bS}-\tilde{\boldsymbol{\delta}})+\boldsymbol{\delta}\overset{a.s.}{\longrightarrow}\boldsymbol{\delta}, since the first term and second terms go almost surely to 𝟎 \boldsymbol{0} : the first by Lemma A.6 and the almost sure boundedness of 𝐒 ¯ \bar{\bS} while the second by the strong law and the fact that 𝑪 ​ 𝐍 ~ + ​ 𝑪 ⊤ ​ 𝑫 ~ \boldsymbol{C}\tilde{\bN}^{+}\boldsymbol{C}^{\top}\tilde{\boldsymbol{D}} is bounded as shown in Lemma A.5 . Suppose finally that Condition 3.1 does not hold. In this case the set { 1 , … , K } \{1,\ldots,K\} can be partitioned into two subsets A 1 A_{1} , A 2 A_{2} so that the limiting vector 𝜹 ~ \tilde{\boldsymbol{\delta}} contains the differences μ i − μ j \mu_{i}-\mu_{j} only if both i , j i,j belong to A 1 A_{1} or to A 2 A_{2} . This means that no difference μ i − μ j \mu_{i}-\mu_{j} with i ∈ A 1 i\in A_{1} , j ∈ A 2 j\in A_{2} can be consistently estimated and thus 𝜹 ^ \widehat{\boldsymbol{\delta}} can not be consistent. Proof of Lemma 4.2 Proof. In the light of Theorem 4.2 , it is sufficient to show ϕ n → ϕ &gt; 0 \phi_{n}\to\phi&gt;0 in probability as n → ∞ n\to\infty . Equivalently the angle between any column of 𝐌 \bM and any column of 𝐗 \bX converges to a strictly positive number. Let 𝐌 i \bM_{i} and 𝐗 i \bX_{i} denote the i t ​ h i^{th} columns of 𝐌 \bM and 𝐗 \bX respectively. The angle between 𝐌 i \bM_{i} and 𝐗 i \bX_{i} is given by ϕ n , i = cos − 1 ⁡ ( | 𝐌 i ⊤ ​ 𝐗 i | ‖ 𝐌 i ‖ ​ ‖ 𝐗 i ‖ ) = cos − 1 ⁡ ( | ( 𝐌 i ‖ 𝐌 i ‖ ) ⊤ ​ 𝐗 i ‖ 𝐗 i ‖ | ) . \displaystyle\phi_{n,i}=\cos^{-1}(\frac{|\bM_{i}^{\top}\bX_{i}|}{\|\bM_{i}\|\|\bX_{i}\|})=\cos^{-1}(|(\frac{\bM_{i}}{\|\bM_{i}\|})^{\top}\frac{\bX_{i}}{\|\bX_{i}\|}|). Note that 𝐌 i / ‖ 𝐌 i ‖ {\bM_{i}}/{\|\bM_{i}\|} and 𝐗 i / ‖ 𝐗 i ‖ {\bX_{i}}/{\|\bX_{i}\|} are normalized vectors. Let 𝐌 i = ( M i , 1 , … , M i , n ) ⊤ \bM_{i}=(M_{i,1},\ldots,M_{i,n})^{\top} and 𝐗 i = ( X i , 1 , … , X i , n ) ⊤ \bX_{i}=(X_{i,1},\ldots,X_{i,n})^{\top} denote the i t ​ h i^{th} columns of 𝐌 \bM and 𝐗 \bX respectively. Then M i ​ j ∈ { − 1 , 0 , 1 } M_{ij}\in\{-1,0,1\} and ( 𝐌 i / ‖ 𝐌 i ‖ ) ⊤ ​ ( 𝐗 i / ‖ 𝐗 i ‖ ) = ∑ j = 1 n M i ​ j ​ X i ​ j ‖ 𝐌 i ‖ ​ ‖ 𝐗 i ‖ \displaystyle({\bM_{i}}/{\|\bM_{i}\|})^{\top}({\bX_{i}}/{\|\bX_{i}\|})=\frac{\sum_{j=1}^{n}M_{ij}X_{ij}}{\|\bM_{i}\|\|\bX_{i}\|} So using Lemma 3.2.4 in Vershynin (2020) we have ( 𝐌 i / ‖ 𝐌 i ‖ ) ⊤ ​ ( 𝐗 i / ‖ 𝐗 i ‖ ) → 0 ({\bM_{i}}/{\|\bM_{i}\|})^{\top}({\bX_{i}}/{\|\bX_{i}\|})\to 0 with probability one as n → ∞ n\to\infty . Therefore ϕ n , i → π / 2 \phi_{n,i}\to\pi/2 with probability one as n → ∞ n\to\infty . Proof of Lemma 4.3 : Proof. Assume the same notations as in the proof of Lemma 4.1 .
